<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01X7s7kpFqb3f8PVxS9VXVmm`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Type--mechanism dissociation and intervention design.}
5	The central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. The binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type rankings require per-domain calibration ($\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}). Of the 60.8\% residual ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}), the explained 39.2\% is comparable to behavioral research norms \citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}; the systematic component reflects clause-level interactions---conversation trajectory, tool-use sequencing, clause$\times$scenario contingencies---that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing (even perfect disambiguation addresses $\leq$22.4\% of variance). Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\kappa = 0.385$, 52\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty (underspecified permission boundaries require subjective judgment even for human raters) rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data. Core conclusions hold without it (Appendix~\ref{app:sans_auth}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
8	This paper identifies causal mechanisms, not deployment-ready effect sizes. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\,pp vs.\ $+$34.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}) confirms directional consistency; the $2.7\times$ compression reflects three measurement factors: (1)~grain (constraint- vs.\ episode-level scoring), (2)~baseline compression (22.7\% vs.\ 6.9\% unambiguous rates), and (3)~interaction depth (single- vs.\ multi-turn). Cross-domain type rankings show modest correlation ($\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}), indicating that type-level findings are domain-contingent. The dissociation depends on mechanism structure, not magnitude, generalizing independently of effect size calibration.
9	
```

</details>

Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\\kappa = 0.385$, 52\\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty (underspecified permission boundaries require subjective judgment even for human raters) rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data. Core conclusions hold without it (Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_auth}).",
    "newString": "The central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. Of the 60.8\\% residual ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}), the systematic component reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing (even perfect disambiguation addresses $\\leq$22.4\\% of variance). Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \\emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \\emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\\kappa = 0.385$, 52\\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data (Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_auth}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Type--mechanism dissociation and intervention design.}\nThe central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. The binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type rankings require per-domain calibration ($\\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}). Of the 60.8\\% residual ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}), the explained 39.2\\% is comparable to behavioral research norms \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}; the systematic component reflects clause-level interactions---conversation trajectory, tool-use sequencing, clause$\\times$scenario contingencies---that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing (even perfect disambiguation addresses $\\leq$22.4\\% of variance). Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \\emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \\emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\\kappa = 0.385$, 52\\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty (underspecified permission boundaries require subjective judgment even for human raters) rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data. Core conclusions hold without it (Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_auth}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ecological validity.}\nThis paper identifies causal mechanisms, not deployment-ready effect sizes. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp vs.\\ $+$34.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}) confirms directional consistency; the $2.7\\times$ compression reflects three measurement factors: (1)~grain (constraint- vs.\\ episode-level scoring), (2)~baseline compression (22.7\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\% unambiguous rates), and (3)~interaction depth (single- vs.\\ multi-turn). Cross-domain type rankings show modest correlation ($\\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), indicating that type-level findings are domain-contingent. The dissociation depends on mechanism structure, not magnitude, generalizing independently of effect size calibration.\n",

A type--mechanism dissociation ($V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument: type predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether}. With $>$89\\% disambiguation neglect, $>$40\\% convergent violations, and 60.8\\% clause-level variance, clause-level policy auditing is the actionable intervention for policy-governed deployments in similar customer-service settings."
        ]

36	We adopt `nocuous' from \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \emph{convergent} rather than \emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\% unanimous)---we term this \emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations (incompleteness highest: 24\%, OR $= 2.61$; subject to information-addition confound).
37	
38	These convergent violations exhibit a \emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:attractor}); conditional precedence is the exception (51.5\% permissive vs.\ 47.1\% restrictive).

    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$).}\n\\label{fig:type_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). The failure mode coder prompt receives the agent trajectory, policy clause, ground truth, and violation assessment, but does \\emph{not} include the ambiguity type label; type is assigned programmatically post hoc. Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Human mechanism coding yields $\\kappa = 0.15$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:human_validation}), concentrated on the spurious compliance vs.\\ structural misparse boundary; collapsing these into a single \\emph{policy misinterpretation} category raises $\\kappa$ to $0.36$. The type--mechanism association reported here ($V = 0.45$) should therefore be interpreted as an upper bound; the directional pattern---type predicts mechanism more strongly than severity---holds under both LLM and collapsed human coding. The following failure mode patterns should therefore be interpreted as consistent LLM categorization tendencies rather than ground-truth mechanism assignments. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\\to$structural misparse, 82.7\\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. Overall awareness is 4.6\\%, clustering by model: GPT-4.1 and Claude show 10--11\\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek exhibit zero across 278 episodes ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}).\n\nWe term this \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending the ambiguity collapse phenomenon documented in static NLP tasks \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic policy execution, where it produces irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} rather than \\emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---we term this \\emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations (incompleteness highest: 24\\%, OR $= 2.61$; subject to information-addition confound).\n\nThese convergent violations exhibit a \\emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attractor}); conditional precedence is the exception (51.5\\% permissive vs.\\ 47.1\\% restrictive).\n\n\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Type--mechanism dissociation and intervention design.}\nThe central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. Of the 60.8\\% residual ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}), the systematic component reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing (even perfect disambiguation addresses $\\leq$22.4\\% of variance).           "+The central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. The 60.8\\% residual ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}) reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing; prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \\emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \\emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\\kappa = 0.385$, 52\\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data (Appendix~\\ref{app:sans_auth}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Ecological validity.}",
          " This paper identifies causal mechanisms, not deployment-ready effect sizes. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp vs.\\ $+$34.0\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}) confirms directional consistency; the $2.7\\times$ compression reflects grain (constraint- vs.\\ episode-level), baseline compression (22.7\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%), and interaction depth differences. Cross-domain type rankings show modest correlation ($\\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_domain}), indicating domain-contingent type-level findings."
        ]
      }
    ],

- "8 Conclusion" header is at the very bottom of page 8!

Page 9: Shows:
- "tion neglect, >40% convergent violations, and 60.8% clause-level variance, clause-level policy auditing is the actionable intervention for policy-governed deployments in similar customer-service settings."


- Followed by Limitations, Data and Code Availability, and References sections

So the Conclusion header sits at the bottom of page 8 with the opening line of text, then the conclusion continues across to page 9 before moving into the remaining sections.

The issue is that the main content spills about 3-4 lines onto page 9, which violates the 8-page limit for EMNLP submissions. Since Limitations and beyond don't count toward that limit, I need to trim the Conclusion section to fit entirely within the 8 pages.

The current Conclusion is already quite dense with four sentences packed with statistical findings and implications. I could condense it by combining some of the key claims—merging the policy ambiguity effect with the mechanism dissociation insight, then consolidating the actionable recommendations into a single closing statement. This would likely save enough space to pull those overflow lines back onto page 8. I'm deciding whether to trim "and 60.8% clause-level variance," to save space, keeping the core findings intact—the 31.0pp violation increase, the type-mechanism dissociation, and the clause-level auditing recommendation. I also need to relocate the page-enlargement command in the conclusion so it applies correctly to the right page. it to apply to page 8 instead, or try putting it in the discussion section.

Actually wait — the \enlargethispage{8\baselineskip} is in conclusion.tex at line 1, BEFORE \section{Conclusion}. When LaTeX processes this, the \enlargethispage command is processed at the point where it appears in the source. Since the conclusion section header ends up at the bottom of page 8, the \enlargethispage command (which is before it) would be on page 8 too. So it IS enlarging page 8 by 8 baselineskips.

BUT I also have \enlargethispage{12\baselineskip} in analysis.tex, which is ALSO on page 8. Can you have two \enlargethispage commands on the same page? Actually, yes — they accumulate. So page 8 is enlarged by 12 + 8 = 20 baselineskips total, which is already quite aggressive.

The real issue is that page 8 has figures 2, 3, and 4 taking up roughly 40-50% of the space at the top, which severely limits how much text can fit regardless of the enlargement. Since the conclusion only spills 3 lines onto page 9, I could just compress it slightly to make everything fit on page 8.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KupETtgNjtmZu8Vr2WMxkq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds 55--87\% contain at least one ambiguity type (dual-judge $\kappa = 0.30$; range reflects union vs.\ intersection; Appendix~\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \emph{divergence}; we retain their harm semantics but document a distinct pattern: LLM agents \emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making the ambiguity harmful through systematic rather than divergent error (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}
13	\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}
14	\label{fig:overview}
15	\end{figure*}
16	
17	We address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.
18	
19	Our results reveal a \emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\,pp in confound-free types), but ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation reframes the taxonomy from a risk classifier to a diagnostic instrument (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
20	
21	Our contributions are: (1)~a matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\,pp in customer-service domains, with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor (22.4\% vs.\ 0.4\% of variance; \S\ref{sec:results}); (2)~an \emph{exploratory type--mechanism dissociation}: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument and motivating clause-level over type-level policy auditing; and (3)~two empirical phenomena---\emph{disambiguation neglect} (4.6\% awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity} (41.3\% convergent violations, 77\% permissive bias)---showing models converge on identical policy violations (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
22

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nA customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.\n\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? ```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points in confound-free types (34.0\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp). However, ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; LLM-coded, human $\kappa = 0.36$ collapsed). This \emph{type--mechanism dissociation} establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument---identifying type-specific failure modes such as structural misparse and boundary violation---rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
3	\end{abstract}
4

```text
1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}
7	\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$).}
8	\label{fig:type_mechanism}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
12	
13	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). The failure mode coder prompt receives the agent trajectory, policy clause, ground truth, and violation assessment, but does \emph{not} include the ambiguity type label; type is assigned programmatically post hoc. Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Human mechanism coding yields $\kappa = 0.15$ (Appendix~\ref{app:human_validation}), concentrated on the spurious compliance vs.\ structural misparse boundary; collapsing these into a single \emph{policy misinterpretation} category raises $\kappa$ to $0.36$. The type--mechanism association reported here ($V = 0.45$) should therefore be interpreted as an upper bound; the directional pattern---type predicts mechanism more strongly than severity---holds under both LLM and collapsed human coding. The following failure mode patterns should therefore be interpreted as consistent LLM categorization tendencies rather than ground-truth mechanism assignments. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\to$structural misparse, 82.7\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.
14	
15	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
16	
17	The five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
18	
19	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
20	
21	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
22	
23	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
24	
25	All dominant modes achieve $\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).
26	
27	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
28	\label{sec:dn}
29	
30	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity. Overall awareness is 4.6\%, clustering by model: GPT-4.1 and Claude show 10--11\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek exhibit zero across 278 episodes ($p = 2.2 \times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}).
31	
32	We term this \emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending the ambiguity collapse phenomenon documented in static NLP tasks \citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic policy execution, where it produces irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.
33	
34	\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}
35	
36	We adopt `nocuous' from \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \emph{convergent} rather than \emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\% unanimous)---we term this \emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations (incompleteness highest: 24\%, OR $= 2.61$; subject to information-addition confound).
37	
38	These convergent violations exhibit a \emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:attractor}); conditional precedence is the exception (51.5\% permissive vs.\ 47.1\% restrictive).
39	
40	\enlargethispage{12\baselineskip}
41	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Type--mechanism dissociation and intervention design.}
5	The central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. The 60.8\% residual ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}) reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing; prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\kappa = 0.385$, 52\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data (Appendix~\ref{app:sans_auth}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Ecological validity.}
8	This paper identifies causal mechanisms, not deployment-ready effect sizes. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\,pp vs.\ $+$34.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}) confirms directional consistency; the $2.7\times$ compression reflects grain (constraint- vs.\ episode-level), baseline compression (22.7\% vs.\ 6.9\%), and interaction depth differences. Cross-domain type rankings show modest correlation ($\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}), indicating domain-contingent type-level findings.
9	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	Our ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\,pp) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; other domains, smaller models, and stochastic sampling remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\kappa = 0.52$; human $\kappa = 0.15$, rising to $0.36$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. At temperature $> 0$, stochastic sampling would likely increase variance in both conditions but is unlikely to eliminate the directional effect, given its magnitude ($+$31.0\,pp confound-free) and cross-model consistency.
5	
6	\section*{Data and Code Availability}
7	The 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \url{https://github.com/anonymous/policy-ambiguity-safety}.
8	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$).}\n\\label{fig:type_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). The failure mode coder prompt receives the agent trajectory, policy clause, ground truth, and violation assessment, but does \\emph{not} include the ambiguity type label; type is assigned programmatically post hoc. Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Human mechanism coding yields $\\kappa = 0.15$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:human_validation}), concentrated on the spurious compliance vs.\\ structural misparse boundary; collapsing these into a single \\emph{policy misinterpretation} category raises $\\kappa$ to $0.36$. The type--mechanism association reported here ($V = 0.45$) should therefore be interpreted as an upper bound; the directional pattern---type predicts mechanism more strongly than severity---holds under both LLM and collapsed human coding. The following failure mode patterns should therefore be interpreted as consistent LLM categorization tendencies rather than ground-truth mechanism assignments. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\\to$structural misparse, 82.7\\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. Overall awareness is 4.6\\%, clustering by model: GPT-4.1 and Claude show 10--11\\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek exhibit zero across 278 episodes ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}).\n\nWe term this \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending the ambiguity collapse phenomenon documented in static NLP tasks \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} to agentic policy execution, where it produces irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} rather than \\emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---we term this \\emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations (incompleteness highest: 24\\%, OR $= 2.61$; subject to information-addition confound).\n\nThese convergent violations exhibit a \\emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attractor}); conditional precedence is the exception (51.5\\% permissive vs.\\ 47.1\\% restrictive).\n\n\\enlargethispage{12\\baselineskip}\n",

The 60.8\\% residual ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}) reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing; prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.36$), confirming that \\emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \\emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder.     "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; other domains, smaller models, and stochastic sampling remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.15$, rising to $0.36$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. At temperature $> 0$, stochastic sampling would likely increase variance in both conditions but is unlikely to eliminate the directional effect, given its magnitude ($+$31.0\\,pp confound-free) and cross-model consistency.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous/policy-ambiguity-safety}.\n",

    "newString": "human $\\kappa = 0.38$ collapsed",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points in confound-free types (34.0\\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\\,pp). However, ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; LLM-coded, human $\\kappa = 0.36$ collapsed). This \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation} establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument---identifying type-specific failure modes such as structural misparse and boundary violation---rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \\citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

### Tool result

_source line 8589 · 2026-05-23T12:36:05.342Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Lgr8sz5pHYHPsFL2EYFDtt`</summary>

```text
753	Human agreement on failure mechanism classification is low ($\kappa = 0.15$; 27.3\% exact match). The primary disagreement axis is between \emph{spurious compliance} (Annotator~1: 48\%) and \emph{structural misparse} (Annotator~2: 41\%)---both describe policy misinterpretation but differ in whether the agent's surface behavior appears compliant. The LLM annotators' higher $\kappa = 0.52$ likely reflects systematic classification conventions rather than superior semantic discrimination.
754	
755	\paragraph{Collapsed-category analysis.}
756	The primary disagreement axis---spurious compliance vs.\ structural misparse---reflects a fuzzy boundary between two forms of policy misinterpretation rather than independent constructs. Merging these into a single \emph{policy misinterpretation} category yields $\kappa = 0.36$ (range $0.34$--$0.39$ across three plausible confusion matrices; 60\% exact match), moving from slight to fair agreement \citep{landis_koch_1977}. This suggests a four-category scheme (policy misinterpretation, information gap-filling, boundary violation, partial compliance) better reflects the annotation task's discriminability.
757	
758	\paragraph{Implications.}
759	Violation detection is reliable across both human and LLM judges. Failure-mode analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}) should be interpreted as reflecting LLM-consistent categorization patterns rather than ground-truth mechanism labels; the type-level patterns (e.g., incompleteness $\to$ gap-filling, authorization scope $\to$ boundary violation) remain informative as relative tendencies even under annotator disagreement.
760	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nA customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.\n\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds 55--87\\% contain at least one ambiguity type (dual-judge $\\kappa = 0.30$; range reflects union vs.\\ intersection; Appendix~\\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \\emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \\emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \\emph{divergence}; we retain their harm semantics but document a distinct pattern: LLM agents \\emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making the ambiguity harmful through systematic rather than divergent error (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.\n\nLLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \\citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \\citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.\n\nOur results reveal a \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\\,pp in confound-free types), but ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation reframes the taxonomy from a risk classifier to a diagnostic instrument (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).\n\nOur contributions are: (1)~a matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\\,pp in customer-service domains, with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor (22.4\\% vs.\\ 0.4\\% of variance; \\S\\ref{sec:results}); (2)~an \\emph{exploratory type--mechanism dissociation}: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument and motivating clause-level over type-level policy auditing; and (3)~two empirical phenomena---\\emph{disambiguation neglect} (4.6\\% awareness) and \\emph{systematic nocuity} (41.3\\% convergent violations, 77\\% permissive bias)---showing models converge on identical policy violations (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points in confound-free types (34.0\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp). However, ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; LLM-coded, human $\kappa = 0.38$ collapsed). This \emph{type--mechanism dissociation} establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument---identifying type-specific failure modes such as structural misparse and boundary violation---rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
3	\end{abstract}
4

      "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points in confound-free types (34.0\\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\\,pp). However, ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail: type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; LLM-coded, human $\\kappa = 0.38$ collapsed). This \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation} establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument---identifying type-specific failure modes such as structural misparse and boundary violation---rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \\citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

```text
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic, estimated from an OpenAI-family ICC replication; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). These proportions are measured at temperature $= 0$; stochastic sampling would inflate the residual but is unlikely to alter the type--clause dominance ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\tau^2$-bench vs.\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\ $+$12.7\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope).
9	
10	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
```

630	
631	The Type~III variance decomposition in \S\ref{sec:results} attributes 60.8\% to residual variance (conversation trajectory and stochasticity). To distinguish systematic-but-unmeasured factors from pure run-to-run noise, we conduct an ICC(1) replication study: 200 clause$\times$model cells, each evaluated five times with independent conversation trajectories ($n = 1{,}000$ total judgments).
632	
633	\paragraph{Overall ICC.} ICC(1) $= 0.749$: 74.9\% of total variance is between-cell (systematic) and 25.1\% is within-cell (stochastic). Under ambiguous conditions specifically, ICC(1) $= 0.782$; under unambiguous conditions, ICC(1) $= 0.404$ (floor effect: most cells are unanimous non-violations).
634	
635	\paragraph{Mapping to residual.} Of the 60.8\% residual in the main decomposition, the ICC estimate partitions this as $\sim$15.2\,pp stochastic (run-to-run noise) and $\sim$45.6\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured (specific conversation trajectories, user-simulator choices, topic interactions). The stochastic share is small enough that it does not threaten the main effects, which operate at 15--49\,pp magnitude.
636	
637	\paragraph{Cell unanimity.} 86.5\% of cells (173/200) produce unanimous outcomes across all five replications---151 unanimous non-violations and 22 unanimous violations. Only 27 cells (13.5\%) show mixed results, confirming that most clause$\times$model combinations produce deterministic outcomes.
638	
639	\paragraph{Per-type ICC.} Incompleteness (ICC $= 0.90$) and conditional precedence (ICC $= 0.86$) are most reproducible; coreferential (ICC $= 0.34$) shows moderate within-cell variance. Authorization scope has the lowest main-study ICC (0.29 from 50 clauses $\times$ 5 models); the replication ICC ($= -0.006$) is uninformative because the sampled 9 auth-scope clauses $\times$ 2 conservative models (Claude, GPT-4.1) produced near-zero violation rates in 34/36 cells (floor effect), leaving no between-cell variance to estimate.
```

28	\paragraph{Perturbation control.} To confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity, we run 300 episodes with non-ambiguity perturbations (synonym substitution, clause reordering). The perturbation effect is $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Within-family reproducibility.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\times$ 2 models $\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider stability is supported by C1 consistency across all seven models.
31	
32	\subsection{Statistical Analysis}

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "fa472dd5-871a-48e3-959c-d9713e9d7463",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Within-family reproducibility.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 models $\\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider stability is supported by C1 consistency across all seven models.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Within-family replicability.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 models $\\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider concordance is moderate (Kendall's $W = 0.398$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}), and all seven models show $\\Delta > 0$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Platform}\n\nWe conduct all experiments on the original release of $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}\n\nFor each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \\emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 conditions $\\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).\n\nClause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\\tau^2$-bench policy text (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\subsection{Models}\n\nWe evaluate five models from four providers spanning two architecture families: GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 (OpenAI, proprietary dense), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthropic, proprietary dense), Qwen3-235B (Alibaba, open-source Mixture-of-Experts), and DeepSeek-V3 (DeepSeek, open-source dense). This selection covers frontier and mid-tier proprietary models alongside open-source alternatives with different training philosophies. All API calls use temperature $= 0$, top\\_p $= 1$, and max\\_tokens $= 4096$, and were made on May 20, 2026.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\nWe use a cross-judge design to mitigate self-evaluation bias: GPT-5.4 judges Claude, GPT-4.1, and Qwen3 traces; GPT-4.1 judges GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek traces. Because our matched-pair design compares ambiguous and unambiguous conditions within the same model--judge pairing, any systematic judge bias cancels in the treatment effect ($\\Delta$pp); the cross-judge asymmetry affects only absolute violation rates, not C1 or C2 (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Judges classify each episode outcome on a four-tier severity scale: none, minor, moderate, and critical. Our primary analysis uses moderate-or-above severity as the violation threshold; minor-only deviations (6.6\\% of episodes) are excluded. Including minor violations (overall rate 49.6\\% vs.\\ 41.0\\%) produces even larger effects without changing any directional conclusion.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-judge calibration.} A dual-judge calibration on 100 stratified episodes yields substantial binary agreement ($\\kappa = 0.653$; weighted severity $\\kappa_w = 0.775$; McNemar $p = 0.606$). Per-type agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope) to $0.875$ (incompleteness) at $n \\approx 17$ per type (Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}).\n\n\\paragraph{Human validation.} Two annotators, blind to LLM labels, labeled 100 stratified episodes. Inter-annotator $\\kappa = 0.798$; human--LLM $\\kappa = 0.733$ ($\\kappa_w = 0.807$), approaching inter-annotator levels and validating the automated pipeline \\citep{zheng_llm_judge_2024}. Per-type estimates ($n \\approx 32$--$34$) have wide CIs (Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}).\n\n\\subsection{Controls}\n\n\\paragraph{Perturbation control.} To confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity, we run 300 episodes with non-ambiguity perturbations (synonym substitution, clause reordering). The perturbation effect is $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\n\\paragraph{Within-family reproducibility.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 models $\\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider stability is supported by C1 consistency across all seven models.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Analysis}\n\nWe use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\\chi^2$ tests with Cram\\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \\citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent policy violations. We use $\\pm 15$\\,pp as a pragmatic threshold: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI: 6.67\\,pp) by $>$2$\\times$, and a per-type difference below this margin would not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. This is a pragmatic default, not a principled domain-anchored threshold; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports sensitivity across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$\\,pp; \\citealp{anvari_lakens_2021}). The margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms the same equivalence structure. We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiple_comparison}); post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2.\n",

The perturbation effect is $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QRa6UcqDGYm3PmNsK3uzGo`</summary>

```text
10	\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}
11	The agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \citep{riskybench_2026}. \citet{mu_can_llms_2023} evaluate rule-following under policy constraints, \citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} examine how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation, and \citet{weng2026beyond} propose policy invariance as a reliability test for LLM safety judges. Complementary prompting approaches target the agent's decision process directly \citep{hahm_causal_2025}. Our work differs by focusing on the \emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.
12	
13	\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}
14	DRIP-R \citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\times$type interaction. Concretely, DRIP-R's correlational design leaves open whether ambiguity \emph{causes} violations or merely co-occurs with harder scenarios; our matched-pair controls ($\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\,pp) isolate the causal contribution. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\tau$-bench \citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. \citet{yu_causal_2026} demonstrate causal safety degradation from tool affordance, a complementary risk vector; \citet{krakovna2020specification} document specification gaming in RL agents, a reward-specification analogue of our policy-specification findings.
```

</details>

    "newString": "AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations.\\linebreak[0]",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design. \\citet{vijayvargiya_ambigswe_2026} show that interactive clarification recovers up to 74\\% of performance lost to underspecification in SE tasks; our results show agents rarely attempt clarification unprompted.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{mu_can_llms_2023} evaluate rule-following under policy constraints, \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} examine how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation, and \\citet{weng2026beyond} propose policy invariance as a reliability test for LLM safety judges. Complementary prompting approaches target the agent's decision process directly \\citep{hahm_causal_2025}. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. Concretely, DRIP-R's correlational design leaves open whether ambiguity \\emph{causes} violations or merely co-occurs with harder scenarios; our matched-pair controls ($\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp) isolate the causal contribution. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations. MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. \\citet{yu_causal_2026} demonstrate causal safety degradation from tool affordance, a complementary risk vector; \\citet{krakovna2020specification} document specification gaming in RL agents, a reward-specification analogue of our policy-specification findings.",
