    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Ambiguity in NLP and requirements engineering.}\nAmbiguity has been studied extensively in both computational linguistics and software engineering. Word sense disambiguation \\citep{navigli_wsd_2009} addresses lexical ambiguity at the token level; our lexical type extends this to policy-specific polysemy. \\citet{li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} propose an 11-type NLP taxonomy covering syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic ambiguity, from which we draw our linguistic-layer types. \\citet{kamath_scope_2024} investigate scopal ambiguity in LLMs, finding systematic preferences for surface scope. In requirements engineering (RE), \\citet{massey_ambiguity_re_2014} classify six ambiguity types in regulatory texts, and \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} introduce the distinction between nocuous ambiguity (where stakeholders diverge on interpretation) and innocuous ambiguity (where they converge). Computational detection of nocuous ambiguity has progressed from heuristic methods \\citep{chantree_2006,willis_nocuous_2008} through NLP-based cross-domain detectors \\citep{ferrari_ambiguity_2019,ferrari_defects_2018} to transformer-based approaches for anaphoric ambiguity \\citep{ezzini_taphsir_2022,ezzini_anaphoric_2022}. \\citet{zhao_nlp4re_2021} survey the broader NLP4RE landscape. Our work extends Berry and Kamsties' framework to LLM agents, where the absence of shared organizational context makes previously innocuous ambiguity nocuous. Our taxonomy serves as an experimental instrument for controlled causal analysis rather than a proposed classification standard.\n\n\\paragraph{Psycholinguistics of ambiguity processing.}\n\\citet{ferreira_good_enough_2002} show that human comprehenders build shallow, ``good-enough'' representations rather than full parses, paralleling our observation that LLM agents adopt surface interpretations. \\citet{frazier_rayner_1982} demonstrate early commitment to a single parse; \\citet{li_garden_path_llm_2024} provide direct evidence that LLMs exhibit analogous garden-path effects. At the pragmatic level, \\citet{hu_pragmatic_2023} and \\citet{ruis_goldilocks_2023} show that LLMs struggle with implicature resolution, defaulting to literal interpretations even when pragmatic inference is required. \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} demonstrate that language models fail to represent ambiguity, collapsing multiple interpretations into a single output; \\citet{gur_arieh_ambiguity_collapse_2026} taxonomize the epistemic risks of this ``ambiguity collapse''; \\citet{sedova_to_know_2024} show analogous self-inconsistencies under entity-type ambiguity. These findings ground our \\emph{disambiguation neglect} result: agents process ambiguous input without meta-cognitive awareness but, unlike humans, lack social channels to recover. \\citet{disambiguation_cqa_survey_2025} survey disambiguation strategies in conversational QA; \\citet{suri_structured_2025} show that structured uncertainty estimation improves coverage by 7--39\\%, while \\citet{sehwag_aspi_2026} demonstrate that clarification-seeking introduces prompt injection vulnerabilities---a tension for disambiguation channel design. \\citet{vijayvargiya_ambigswe_2026} show that interactive clarification recovers up to 74\\% of performance lost to underspecification in SE tasks; our results show agents rarely attempt clarification unprompted.\n\n\\paragraph{Agent safety evaluation.}\nThe agent safety evaluation landscape includes benchmarks for risk awareness \\citep{rjudge_2024}, tool-use safety \\citep{toolemu_2024}, indirect prompt injection \\citep{injecagent_2024}, safe task planning \\citep{yin_safeagentbench_2025}, policy-invisible violations \\citep{phantompolicy_2026}, comprehensive real-world agent safety \\citep{vijayvargiya_openagentsafety_2026}, trajectory-level diagnosis \\citep{atbench_2026}, and deployment-condition probing \\citep{riskybench_2026}. \\citet{mu_can_llms_2023} evaluate rule-following under policy constraints, \\citet{tamkin_evaluating_2023} examine how language models navigate policy-governed decisions, \\citet{shieldagent_2025} extract formal safety rules from policy documents, and AgentAuditor \\citep{agentauditor_2025} achieves human-level safety evaluation, and \\citet{weng2026beyond} propose policy invariance as a reliability test for LLM safety judges. Complementary prompting approaches target the agent's decision process directly \\citep{hahm_causal_2025}. Our work differs by focusing on the \\emph{source specification} rather than the agent's execution: we measure how ambiguity in the policy itself causes safety violations, complementing work that assumes clean policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Policy specification for agents.}\nDRIP-R \\citep{drip_r_2026} is the closest concurrent work: it benchmarks 400 policy-ambiguous retail scenarios across five RE ambiguity types, measuring decision quality rather than safety violations. Our work differs in three respects: (1) our matched-pair design establishes causality rather than correlating ambiguity presence with outcomes; (2) we measure safety violations rather than decision quality, enabling failure mechanism analysis; (3) our two-layer taxonomy adds linguistic-layer types, and we quantify the model$\\times$type interaction. Concretely, DRIP-R's correlational design leaves open whether ambiguity \\emph{causes} violations or merely co-occurs with harder scenarios; our matched-pair controls ($\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$\\,pp) isolate the causal contribution. We map DRIP-R's five RE ambiguity categories to our six-type taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:drip_mapping}. PolicyBank \\citep{policybank_2026} studies iterative policy refinement, closing 82\\% of the gap to a human oracle. AgentPex \\citep{agentpex_2026} detects procedural violations in agentic traces, finding that 83\\% of perfect-outcome traces contain violations.\\linebreak[0] MANTRA \\citep{mantra_2026} synthesizes SMT-validated compliance benchmarks from procedural manuals. $\\tau$-bench \\citep{tau_bench_2024} and its successor $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025} provide the conversational agent evaluation platforms. \\citet{yu_causal_2026} demonstrate causal safety degradation from tool affordance, a complementary risk vector; \\citet{krakovna2020specification} document specification gaming in RL agents, a reward-specification analogue of our policy-specification findings.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EKMqYkFWWHAcx7zTP5ofza`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic, estimated from an OpenAI-family ICC replication; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). These proportions are measured at temperature $= 0$; stochastic sampling would inflate the residual but is unlikely to alter the type--clause dominance ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\tau^2$-bench vs.\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\ $+$12.7\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope).
9	
10	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.

      "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (of which $\\sim$15\\% is stochastic and $\\sim$46\\% systematic, estimated from an OpenAI-family ICC replication; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). These proportions are measured at temperature $= 0$; stochastic sampling would inflate the residual but is unlikely to alter the type--clause dominance ordering (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\\tau^2$-bench vs.\\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\\ $+$12.7\\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope).\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\\tau^2$-bench vs.\\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\\ $+$12.7\\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope).\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for construct subjectivity analysis. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing across seven margins (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) reveals a margin-dependent structure: incompleteness separates at all margins; coreferential separates above $\\pm 12$\\,pp; the remaining four types form a stable mid-tier cluster at $\\pm 10$\\,pp and above. The $V$ comparison ($0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}).\n\nAn important qualification: three-family cross-judge validation yields concordant type rankings ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}), but two-group rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope$^\\ddagger$. Excluding it, $\\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); the remaining five types preserve the partial ordering. Authorization scope is diagnostically distinct, with lower agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) reflecting construct subjectivity---whether \\emph{investigating} an unauthorized request constitutes a violation.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. ```text
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}
11	\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates. Clause identity explains 22.4\% of variance (of which only 0.4\% is between types), while condition (ambiguous vs.\ unambiguous) explains 15.8\%, model 1.0\%, and residual 60.8\%. Measured at temperature $= 0$.}
12	\label{fig:variance_decomposition}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), condition 15.8\%, model 1.0\%, and residual 60.8\% ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\tau^2$-bench vs.\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\ $+$12.7\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope).
16	
17	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
18	
19	\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}
20

49	Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}).
50	
51	An important qualification: three-family cross-judge validation yields concordant type rankings ($\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}), but two-group rankings are discordant ($\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope$^\ddagger$. Excluding it, $\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); the remaining five types preserve the partial ordering. Authorization scope is diagnostically distinct, with lower agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$) reflecting construct subjectivity---whether \emph{investigating} an unauthorized request constitutes a violation.
52	
53	Incompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).

C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates. Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\\tau^2$-bench vs.\\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\\ $+$12.7\\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates. Clause identity explains 22.4\\% of variance (of which only 0.4\\% is between types), while condition (ambiguous vs.\\ unambiguous) explains 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\%. Measured at temperature $= 0$.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\\tau^2$-bench vs.\\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\\ $+$12.7\\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope).\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for construct subjectivity analysis. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing across seven margins (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) reveals a margin-dependent structure: incompleteness separates at all margins; coreferential separates above $\\pm 12$\\,pp; the remaining four types form a stable mid-tier cluster at $\\pm 10$\\,pp and above. The $V$ comparison ($0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}).\n\nAn important qualification: three-family cross-judge validation yields concordant type rankings ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}), but two-group rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope$^\\ddagger$. Excluding it, $\\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); the remaining five types preserve the partial ordering. Authorization scope is diagnostically distinct, with lower agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) reflecting construct subjectivity---whether \\emph{investigating} an unauthorized request constitutes a violation.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$), reflecting differential safety training; incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific (\\S\\ref{sec:c4}).}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory. The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope (Claude 12\\% vs.\\ GPT-5.4/DeepSeek $>$60\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap}); excluding it, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}). Cross-judge validation confirms model rankings (Fleiss' $\\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}).\n\n",

620	\section{Variance Decomposition}
621	\label{app:variance_fig}
622	\label{app:residual}
623	
624	\begin{figure}[ht]
625	\centering
626	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}
627	\caption{Variance decomposition of violation outcomes (Type III SS). Clause wording (22.4\%) is the largest actionable component; type membership explains only 0.4\%. The dashed line separates actionable factors (39.2\%) from residual variance (60.8\%, of which $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic, OpenAI-family estimate).}
628	\label{fig:variance_decomposition}
629	\end{figure}
630	
631	The 6.9\% unambiguous-condition violation rate reflects baseline policy-following errors unrelated to ambiguity, consistent with $\tau^2$-bench's reported error rates.
632	
633	The Type~III variance decomposition in \S\ref{sec:results} attributes 60.8\% to residual variance (conversation trajectory and stochasticity). To distinguish systematic-but-unmeasured factors from pure run-to-run noise, we conduct an ICC(1) replication study: 200 clause$\times$model cells, each evaluated five times with independent conversation trajectories ($n = 1{,}000$ total judgments).
634	
635	\paragraph{Overall ICC.} ICC(1) $= 0.749$: 74.9\% of total variance is between-cell (systematic) and 25.1\% is within-cell (stochastic). Under ambiguous conditions specifically, ICC(1) $= 0.782$; under unambiguous conditions, ICC(1) $= 0.404$ (floor effect: most cells are unanimous non-violations).
636	
637	\paragraph{Mapping to residual.} Of the 60.8\% residual in the main decomposition, the ICC estimate partitions this as $\sim$15.2\,pp stochastic (run-to-run noise) and $\sim$45.6\,pp systematic-but-unmeasured (specific conversation trajectories, user-simulator choices, topic interactions). The stochastic share is small enough that it does not threaten the main effects, which operate at 15--49\,pp magnitude.
638

     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points in confound-free types (34.0\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp). However, ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency ($V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; human $\kappa = 0.38$ collapsed). This \emph{type--mechanism dissociation} establishes the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

     \paragraph{Type--mechanism dissociation and intervention design.}
     The central finding is a type--mechanism dissociation: type predicts failure mechanism more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$), reframing the taxonomy from risk classifier to diagnostic instrument. The 60.8\% residual ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}) reflects clause-level interactions that type-level taxonomies cannot capture, motivating clause-level auditing; prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Violation severity achieves perfect human $\kappa = 1.00$ while mechanism classification reaches only $\kappa = 0.15$ (collapsed: $0.38$), confirming that \emph{what} constitutes a violation is clear but \emph{why} it occurs is interpretively harder. Authorization scope is a construct outlier ($\kappa = 0.385$, 52\,pp range)---its low inter-judge reliability reflects genuine construct difficulty rather than taxonomy invalidity; the core type--mechanism mapping (boundary violation, 5/5 models) remains the most unanimous in our data (Appendix~\ref{app:sans_auth}).

     \paragraph{Ecological validity.}
     This paper identifies causal mechanisms, not deployment-ready effect sizes. The SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\,pp vs.\ $+$34.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}) confirms directional consistency; the $2.7\times$ compression reflects grain (constraint- vs.\ episode-level), baseline compression (22.7\% vs.\ 6.9\%), and interaction depth differences. Cross-domain type rankings show modest correlation ($\rho = 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_domain}), indicating domain-contingent type-level findings.
     ```

C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates. Clause identity explains 22.4\\% of variance (of which only 0.4\\% is between types), while condition (ambiguous vs.\\ unambiguous) explains 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\%. Measured at temperature $= 0$.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition. Incompleteness is fundamentally confounded: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content (specifying what to do in the absent case), introducing an information-type confound that token-count matching cannot address. We therefore exclude incompleteness from our primary causal claim: the confound-free effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$). This confound may partially explain the $\\tau^2$-bench vs.\\ SOPBench effect gap ($+$34.0 vs.\\ $+$12.7\\,pp), as SOPBench uses naturally occurring policies where incompleteness confounds are diluted. The confound-free range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope).\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; the raw NMI ratio is inflated by target cardinality asymmetry; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for construct subjectivity analysis. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing across seven margins (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}) reveals a margin-dependent structure: incompleteness separates at all margins; coreferential separates above $\\pm 12$\\,pp; the remaining four types form a stable mid-tier cluster at $\\pm 10$\\,pp and above. The $V$ comparison ($0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}).\n\nAn important qualification: three-family cross-judge validation yields concordant type rankings ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}), but two-group rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope$^\\ddagger$. Excluding it, $\\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); the remaining five types preserve the partial ordering. Authorization scope is diagnostically distinct, with lower agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$) reflecting construct subjectivity---whether \\emph{investigating} an unauthorized request constitutes a violation.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).
